Proof of
Theorem 4.1

Proof.

ACs over the empty graph
\emptyset
in particular express so-called graph properties. Graph properties formulated this way have the same expressive power as first-order logic (FOL) on graphs
9
9

9

FOL on graphs is standard first-order logic with two additional built-in predicates:
Node(n)
-to state that
n
is a node and
Edge(e,n,n^{\prime})
to state that
e
is an edge from
n
to
n^{\prime}
.
as shown in
[
14
]
. This means that we can express any graph property
equivalently using a first-order formula. For the same reason, we can state any graph property for graphs without isolated nodes using a first-order formula (i.e., any graph property that, in particular, implies that the given graph has no isolated nodes).

Now consider the following two rules
\rho_{1}=\langle\emptyset\leftarrow\emptyset\rightarrow\emptyset,c\rangle
and

\rho_{2}=\langle\emptyset\leftarrow\emptyset\rightarrow 1_{N},\mathit{true}\rangle
, with
1_{N}
the graph consisting of an isolated node and
c
some property (expressible using ACs over the empty graph) about graphs without isolated nodes. That is, the first rule can be applied to a graph
G
, if
G\models c
, and it leaves
G
unchanged; and the second rule, which is always applicable, adds an isolated node to
G
. Thus the set of transformation pairs associated to these rules is

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\{G\Longleftarrow_{\rho_{1}}G\Longrightarrow_{\rho_{2}}G\oplus 1_{N}\mid G\models c\}
. Note that all the transformation pairs in
\mathcal{S}_{0}
are AC conflicts, since
G\oplus 1_{N}
does not satisfy
c
having an isolated node, which means that the set of conflicts of
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
is precisely
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. In particular, for any graph
G
either
G\models c
10
10

10

A graph property is an application condition over the empty graph
\emptyset
(or, in the general case, the initial object in the category of graphical structures considered), thus composed of literals of the form
c=\exists(\emptyset\rightarrow G^{\prime},c^{\prime})
. In particular, we say that
G\models c
if
i_{G}\models c
with
i_{G}
the unique morphism from
\emptyset
to
G
.
and both rules can be applied to
G
in a unique way (since there is a unique match
h:\emptyset\rightarrow G
), or
G\not\models c
such that
\rho_{1}
cannot be applied. This means that, for any
G
, there is at most one transformation pair
G\Longleftarrow_{\rho_{1}}G\Longrightarrow_{\rho_{2}}G\oplus 1_{N}
starting from
G
. Consequently, if
G
and
G^{\prime}
satisfy
c
, any morphism
h:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
defines an extension diagram between their associated transformations. For these reasons, even if it is an abuse of notation, given sets of transformation pairs
\mathcal{S}_{0}
(
\mathcal{S}
), we write
G\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}
) meaning
G\Longleftarrow_{\rho_{1}}G\Longrightarrow_{\rho_{2}}G\oplus 1_{N}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}
).

Now let us assume that a finite set
\mathcal{S}
of conflicts for rules
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
exists that is complete w.r.t. parallel dependence.
This means that
G\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
if and only if there is a

G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
and a morphism
h:G^{\prime}\rightarrow G
. We know, by the property of epi-mono factorization, that any morphism
h:G^{\prime}\rightarrow G
can be decomposed into
h=m\circ e
with
m
mono and
e
epi. Moreover, since
G^{\prime}
is assumed to be finite, there is a finite number of epimorphisms whose source is
G^{\prime}
. Let
Epi_{G^{\prime}}
be the set
\{G^{\prime\prime}\mid\text{there\ is\ an\ epimorphism\ }e:G^{\prime}\rightarrow G^{\prime\prime}\}
, then we would have that
G\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
if and only if there is a

G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
, a
G^{\prime\prime}\in Epi_{G^{\prime}}
and a monomorphism
m:G^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow G
.
Note that, by definition of satisfaction (cf.
1
), the property that there is a monomorphism
m:G^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow G
is equivalent to

G\models\exists(\emptyset\rightarrow G^{\prime\prime},\mathit{true})
. Therefore
G\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
if and only if there is a

G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
, and a
G^{\prime\prime}\in Epi_{G^{\prime}}
such that
G\models\exists(\emptyset\rightarrow G^{\prime\prime},\mathit{true})
. But this means that
G\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
if and only if there is a

G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
such that
G\models\big(\bigvee_{G^{\prime\prime}\in Epi_{G^{\prime}}}\exists(\emptyset\rightarrow G^{\prime\prime},\mathit{true})\big)
, or equivalently
G\models c^{\prime}
, where c’ is the condition

c^{\prime}=\big(\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}G^{\prime\prime}\in Epi_{G^{\prime}}\\
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}\exists(\emptyset\rightarrow G^{\prime\prime},\mathit{true})\big).

This means however that
c
and
c^{\prime}
are logically equivalent, but this is a contradiction, since it is not possible to represent any arbitrarily complex first-order formula in terms of a finite disjunction of existential atoms. Therefore, our assumption was wrong and
\mathcal{S}
is in general infinite.∎∎

Proof of
Theorem 4.2

Proof.

Given some initial conflict
stp_{K}:\langle tp_{K},\mathrm{ac}_{K},\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}\rangle
, we have that its matches
\langle o_{1},o_{2}\rangle
are in
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}
, since either
tp_{K}
is an initial conflict or the initial parallel independent transformation pair for the plain rules
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Note that for plain initial conflicts it is shown in
[
23
]
that their matches belong to
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}
, and the coproduct morphisms also belong to
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}
because of uniqueness of the
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}-\mathcal{M}
pair factorization and the coproduct property.
We moreover have that
stp_{K}
is in particular a conflict-inducing symbolic transformation pair. This means that there exists a conflicting pair
tp_{G}:H_{1}\Leftarrow_{\rho_{1},m_{1}}G\Rightarrow_{\rho_{2},m_{2}}H_{2}
in its unfolding
\mathcal{U}(stp_{K})
. Consequently, from the corresponding extension diagram with the extension morphism
m:K\rightarrow G
we can derive directly that
m\circ o_{i}
for
i=1,2
satisfy the gluing conditions. Moreover, because of
1
and the fact that
m\circ o_{i}\models\mathrm{ac}_{L_{i}}
for
i=1,2
we know that
m\models\mathrm{ac}_{K}
.

Finally, we have to show that
m\models\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
. Assume that
tp_{K}
is an initial conflict for the plain rules
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. In this case
\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
is always true such that
m\models\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
. Assume that
tp_{K}
equals the initial parallel independent transformation pair
tp_{L_{1}+L_{2}}
as in
Figure 3
. We know that
tp_{G}
is conflicting. It cannot be a use-delete/delete-use conflict, since this would be a contradiction with
tp_{L_{1}+L_{2}}
being parallel independent for plain rules. Thus
tp_{G}
is an AC conflict. This means that either
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}}
or
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{2}}
are not satisfied by the extended matches into
H_{2}
and
H_{1}
, respectively. Then it follows because of
1
,
2
, and the fact that diagonal morphisms for plain parallel independence are unique w.r.t. making the corresponding triangles commute that
m\models\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}=\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{L_{1}+L_{2}}
with
\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{L_{1}+L_{2},d_{12}}=\mathrm{L}(p^{*}_{2},\mathrm{Shift}(c_{2}{\circ}d_{12},\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}}))
and
\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{L_{1}+L_{2},d_{21}}=\mathrm{L}(p^{*}_{1},\mathrm{Shift}(c_{1}{\circ}d_{21},\mathrm{ac}_{L_{2}}))
.
∎∎

Proof of
Theorem 4.3

Proof.

Given a parallel dependent pair of transformations
tp_{G}:H_{1}\Leftarrow_{\rho_{1},m_{1}}G\Rightarrow_{\rho_{2},m_{2}}H_{2}
we need to show that some initial conflict via rules
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
exists that can be embedded into
tp_{G}
via some extension morphism
m:K\rightarrow G
with
m\models\mathrm{ac}_{K}\wedge\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
.

Assume that
tp_{G}
is a use-delete/delete-use conflict. Then
tp_{G}
is also a use-delete/delete-use conflict as AC-disregarding transformation pair. This means that an initial conflict
tp_{K}
for the plain rules
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
exists according to
Theorem 3.2
that can be embedded via some extension morphism
m:K\rightarrow G
into
tp_{G}
as AC-disregarding transformation pair. The symbolic transformation pair
stp_{K}:\langle tp_{K},\mathrm{ac}_{K},\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}\rangle
is obviously conflict-inducing. We moreover show that
m\models\mathrm{ac}_{K}\wedge\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
. It follows that
m\models\mathrm{ac}_{K}
because of
1
and the fact that the matches of
tp_{G}
satisfy
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}}
and
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{2}}
. Moreover
m\models\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
since
tp_{K}
is an initial conflict (i.e. delete-use) for the plain rules
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
such that
\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
is true.

Assume that
tp_{G}
is not a use-delete/delete-use conflict, but it is an AC conflict. Since
tp_{G}
is not a use-delete/delete-use conflict we know that it is parallel independent as AC-disregarding transformation pair. This means that the initial parallel independent transformation pair
tp_{L_{1}+L_{2}}:R_{1}+L_{2}\Leftarrow_{p_{1},i_{1}}L_{1}+L_{2}\Rightarrow_{p_{2},i_{2}}L_{1}+R_{2}
for the plain rules
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
can be embedded via extension morphism
m:L_{1}+L_{2}\rightarrow G
into
tp_{G}
as AC-disregarding transformation pair (as illustrated in
Figure 3
). The symbolic transformation pair
stp_{L_{1}+L_{2}}:\langle tp_{L_{1}+L_{2}},\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}+L_{2}},\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{L_{1}+L_{2}}\rangle
is obviously conflict-inducing. We moreover show that
m\models\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}+L_{2}}\wedge\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{L_{1}+L_{2}}
. It follows that
m\models\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}+L_{2}}
because of
1
and the fact that the matches of
tp_{G}
satisfy
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}}
and
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{2}}
. Moreover
m\models\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{L_{1}+L_{2}}
with
\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{L_{1}+L_{2},d_{12}}=\mathrm{L}(p^{*}_{2},\mathrm{Shift}(c_{2}{\circ}d_{12},\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}}))
and
\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{L_{1}+L_{2},d_{21}}=\mathrm{L}(p^{*}_{1},\mathrm{Shift}(c_{1}{\circ}d_{21},\mathrm{ac}_{L_{2}}))

because of
1
,
2
, the fact that diagonal morphisms for plain parallel independence are unique w.r.t. making the corresponding triangles commute, and the fact that
tp_{G}
is AC conflicting (i.e. either
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}}
or
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{2}}
are not satisfied by the extended matches into
H_{2}
and
H_{1}
, respectively). ∎∎

Proof of
2

Proof.

Assume that there exists such a set
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
. Let
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}^{pl}
be the equally sized set of plain transformation pairs via the rules
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
derived from
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
by extracting merely the corresponding plain transformation pairs. Let
\mathcal{K}
be the set of initial conflicts for the plain rules
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
that did not lead to an use-delete/delete-use initial conflict for the rules
\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\rangle
, since their corresponding symbolic transformation pair is not conflict-inducing (because its unfolding is empty). We start with showing that the set
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}^{pl}\cup\mathcal{K}
is complete w.r.t. parallel dependence for the plain rules
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
. Given some conflict
tp
via
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
, then its initial conflict either belongs to
\mathcal{K}
, or not. Assume that it does not belong to
K
. We know by
Theorem 3.2
that some initial conflict via
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
can be embedded into
tp
that leads to some symbolic transformation pair
stp
in
\mathcal{S}
. We know by
14
that then there exists a conflicting transformation pair via
\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\rangle
. Since
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
is complete w.r.t. parallel dependence for
\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\rangle
, there needs to exist indeed a transformation pair in
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}^{pl}
that can be embedded into
tp
.

Now let
\mathcal{P}
be the set of all use-delete/delete-use initial conflicts for
\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\rangle
in
\mathcal{S}
. We continue by assuming the following three cases: the size of
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
is strictly smaller, equal or strictly larger than the size of
\mathcal{P}
.

In the first case, we assume that the size of
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
is strictly smaller than the size of
\mathcal{P}
. We argue that the size of
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}^{pl}\cup\mathcal{K}
is then also strictly smaller than the size of the set of initial conflicts
\mathcal{I}
for
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
. Note that
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{K})\cup\mathcal{K}
. Now
(\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{K})
consists of all initial conflicts for
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
that lead to an initial conflict for
\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\rangle
. This means that the size of
(\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{K})
equals the size of
\mathcal{P}
. This contradicts with
1
, since we have found a set
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}^{pl}\cup\mathcal{K}
that has smaller size than
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{K})\cup\mathcal{K}
, but is still complete w.r.t. parallel dependence for
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
.

In the second case, we assume that the size of
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
equals the size of
\mathcal{P}
. Since
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
has size strictly smaller than the size of
\mathcal{S}
, it holds that
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{P}\cup\{stp_{L_{1}+L_{2}}\}
(by definition
\mathcal{S}
contains at least
\mathcal{P}
). This means that there exists at least one conflicting transformation pair
tp
for
\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\rangle
such that the initial parallel independent transformation pair
tp_{L_{1}+L_{2}}
can be embedded into
tp
. Consequently,
tp
is in particular not a use-delete/delete-use conflict, but an AC conflict. Since
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
is complete w.r.t. parallel dependence for
\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\rangle
it needs to be possible to embed a symbolic transformation pair
stp_{K}
from
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
into
tp
. Note that the plain transformation pair
tp_{K}
underlying
stp_{K}
must be parallel independent, since
tp
as plain transformation pair is parallel independent. This is because it is possible embed the plain parallel independent transformation pair
tp_{L_{1}+L_{2}}
into
tp
that then by initiality can be embedded also into
tp_{K}
. From there it is easy to argue that then also
tp_{K}
must be parallel independent. Now
\mathcal{S}^{\prime pl}
necessarily contains this parallel independent plain pair of transformations
tp_{K}
. Consequently
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}^{pl}
minus this transformation pair
tp_{K}
and together with
\mathcal{K}
would still be complete for the plain rules w.r.t. parallel dependence. The size of
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}^{pl}\setminus\{tp_{K}\}\cup\mathcal{K}
is strictly smaller than the size of
(I\setminus\mathcal{K})\cup\mathcal{K}
. Consequently, we have again found a contradiction with
1
.

In the third case, we assume that the size of
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
is strictly larger than the size of
\mathcal{P}
. This contradicts our assumption, since the size of
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
cannot be strictly smaller than the size of
\mathcal{S}
, which at least contains
\mathcal{P}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 5.1

Proof.

Because each disjunctive unfolding of a regular initial conflict consists of a finite number of elements (see finite index set
1
), the set
\cup_{stp\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{U^{D}}(stp)
is finite as soon as the set
\mathcal{S}
of all initial conflicts is finite.

We now show that the set
\cup_{stp\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{U^{D}}(stp)
(consisting of concrete transformation pairs) is also
\mathcal{M}
-complete w.r.t. parallel dependence.
From
Theorem 4.3
we know that the set of initial conflicts
S
(consisting of symbolic transformation pairs) is complete w.r.t. parallel dependence. This means that there exists some
stp_{K}:\langle tp_{K},\mathrm{ac}_{K},\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}\rangle
with AC-disregarding transformation pair
tp_{K}:P_{1}\Leftarrow_{\rho_{1},o_{1}}K\Rightarrow_{\rho_{2},o_{2}}P_{2}
from
S
that can be embedded into
tp_{G}
via some extension morphism
m:K\rightarrow G
with
m\models\mathrm{ac}_{K}\wedge\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
.

Consequently, since
m\models\mathrm{ac}_{K}\wedge\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
we know that because of having only regular initial conflicts
m\models\vee_{i\in I}\exists(a_{i}:K\rightarrow C_{i},c_{i})
. This means that
m\models\exists(a_{i}:K\rightarrow C_{i},c_{i})
for some
i\in I
meaning that there exists some
q_{i}:C_{i}\rightarrow G\in\mathcal{M}
such that
q_{i}\models c_{i}
and
q_{i}\circ a_{i}=m
. Because of the Restriction Theorem for plain rules
[
5
]
and the fact that
q_{i}
is in
\mathcal{M}
we know that there exists a pair of plain transformations via matches
a_{i}\circ o_{1}
and
a_{i}\circ o_{2}
that can be embedded into
tp_{G}
via extension morphism
q_{i}
.
Now we have to show that the matches
a_{i}\circ o_{1}
and
a_{i}\circ o_{2}
of this transformation pair
tp_{C_{i}}
indeed satisfy the conditions
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}}
and
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{2}}
, respectively. Moreover, we argue that the transformation pair
tp_{C_{i}}
is conflicting. To this extent, consider the identity morphism
id_{C_{i}}
satisfying trivially
c_{i}
. Consequently,
a_{i}\models\exists(a_{i}:K\rightarrow C_{i},c_{i})
, and because of regularity it follows that
a_{i}\models\mathrm{ac}_{K}\wedge\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
. By the Embedding Theorem
[
7
,
8
]
it then follows that we indeed obtain a pair of transformations with
a_{i}\circ o_{1}
and
a_{i}\circ o_{2}
satisfying the rule ACs
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{1}}
and
\mathrm{ac}_{L_{2}}
, since
a_{i}\models\mathrm{ac}_{K}
making it AC-consistent for both AC-disregarding transformations in
tp_{K}
indeed. Moreover, because of Lemma 6.2 (characterization of parallel dependency with ACs) in
[
7
,
8
]

tp_{C_{i}}
is also parallel dependent, since
a_{i}\models\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
.

Since pushouts and pushout complements are unique up to isomorphism this pair of transformations
tp_{C_{i}}
(built for the matches
a_{i}\circ o_{1}
and
a_{i}\circ o_{2}
) is indeed equivalent to some transformation pair from
\mathcal{U^{D}}(stp_{K})
. As a consequence we have indeed found an extension diagram embedding
tp_{C_{i}}:D_{1,i}\Leftarrow_{\rho_{1},a_{i}\circ o_{1}}C_{i}\Rightarrow_{\rho_{2},a_{i}\circ o_{2}}D_{2,i}
in
\mathcal{U^{D}}(stp_{K})
into
tp_{G}
via
q_{i}
.
∎∎

Proof of
Theorem 5.2

Proof.

This follows directly from
5
and the constructions

]
related to

and
1
. In particular,
\mathrm{ac}_{K}
(arising from shifting each rule NAC over the match morphisms into

becomes true or consists of a (non-empty) disjunction of PACs. We obtain by shifting (using

in the case
\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}
becomes true) a condition that is equivalent to a disjunction of literals of the form
\exists(a_{i}:K\rightarrow C_{i},\wedge_{q\in Q}\neg\exists n_{q})
.
∎∎

Proof of
Theorem 5.3

Proof.

Recall that an initial conflict
stp_{K}:\langle tp_{K},\mathrm{ac}_{K},\mathrm{ac}^{*}_{K}\rangle
consists in particular of an initial conflict for plain rules or of an initial parallel independent pair of transformations for plain rules. Having rules with NACs only, we can unfold such an initial conflict into a set of conflicting transformations
tp_{C_{i}}
with each conflict stemming from one literal in the finite disjunction
\vee_{i\in I}\exists(a_{i}:K\rightarrow C_{i},c_{i})
with
c_{i}
a condition of the form
\wedge_{q\in Q}\neg\exists n_{q}
.
When extending the initial parallel independent pair via some
a_{i}:L_{1}+L_{2}\rightarrow C_{i}
, the corresponding transformation pair remains plain parallel independent such that we in particular obtain a critical pair satisfying (1.b) or (2.b) according to
16
.
Moreover we know that
(q_{12},m^{\prime}_{2})
and
(q_{21},m^{\prime}_{1})
belong to
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}
by construction and we know that
tp_{C_{i}}
is AC-conflicting indeed.
In case that we extend an initial conflict for the plain rules to a real conflict for the rules with NACs, we obtain a critical pair either satisfying (1.a) or (2.a) according to
16
in case no additional context is added by the positive application condition
a_{i}
stemming from the disjunction
\vee_{i\in I}\exists(a_{i}:K\rightarrow C_{i},c_{i})
in the unfolding, or satisfying (1.b) or (2.b) as in the previous case.
∎∎
